KIMI Kimi Code this is gonna be a very heavy and complex task i'm thinking of creating my own edge ai inference engine like that of the mlc engines... now consider for example the runtime for a litert js model, litertlm model and all... can i simulate the same buffer manipulations, gpu shardings and all using my engine?? of course is should be able to.. but how to get the architecture and configure this.. i have two options to create the engine.. deno and Bun.. both have ffi api exposure, which i can use to save the model weights, create sharding gpus and load the buffers and do the math computations on the GPU.. which is better bun or Deno?? I went through both, the main difference I saw was that Deno had a KV dictionary that was configurable and all.. i'm thinking this is for the KV caching whereas for the BUN i couldn't find that.. also with DENO i can see so many examples and libraries.. but in BUN i saw C compiler and transpilers...which is better??? how would i be implementing the engine... it will be for cross platform.. so i will need a core engine and then web, android, ios, Linux, Windows and MacOs sublibraries Building an edge AI inference engine is a massive, highly rewarding engineering challenge. To simulate the buffer manipulations, GPU sharding, and memory architectures you see in MLC-LLM or LiteRT, you need precise control over the hardware abstraction layer. Before diving into the architecture, we need to clear up a crucial terminology overlap regarding Deno and Transformers. The "KV" False Cognate Deno KV is a persistent, NoSQL key-value database (backed by SQLite locally or FoundationDB in the cloud). It is used for standard application state storage, much like Redis. A Transformer KV Cache, on the other hand, is a high-bandwidth, volatile memory buffer residing directly in GPU VRAM. It stores the calculated Key and Value vectors of previous tokens during autoregressive generation so the attention mechanism doesn't have to recompute them from scratch (turning an O(N 2 ) operation into O(N)). You cannot use Deno KV for transformer KV caching. The latency of crossing the FFI/disk boundary would cripple your token generation speed. Your KV cache must be manually allocated GPU buffers (GPUBuffer in WebGPU or VkBuffer in Vulkan). Bun vs. Deno for Edge AI If you are strictly choosing between Bun and Deno to orchestrate your engine: Deno is the better choice for prototyping an Edge AI engine. Deno natively ships with the WebGPU API baked into the runtime (re-stabilized in v1.39). This means you can write WGSL compute shaders directly in TypeScript, map GPU memory, and execute tensor math without writing any C++ or touching FFI. Bun has significantly faster raw FFI overhead (bun:ffi is incredibly optimized), but it lacks built-in WebGPU. You would have to write your GPU interface in C/C++ and bind it manually. However, neither Bun nor Deno is the right choice for the core of a cross-platform mobile engine. JavaScript runtimes are too heavy to embed as the base layer of a native Android or iOS application. They are designed for servers and desktops. The Cross-Platform Architecture Blueprint If you want true cross-platform deployment (Web, Android, iOS, Windows, macOS, Linux) with sub-libraries, you need to follow the architecture used by MLC, llama.cpp, and ExecuTorch. You build the core in a low-level language and expose it via bindings: The Core Engine (C++ or Rust) Your core handles memory allocation, the computation graph, tensor parallelization, and the KV Cache (using techniques like PagedAttention). Recommendation: Use Rust with the wgpu crate or C++ with Google's Dawn (the WebGPU C++ implementation). Why WebGPU? WebGPU isn't just for the browser. As a native abstraction layer, it automatically translates your WGSL shaders into Metal (for iOS/Mac), Vulkan (for Android/Linux), and DirectX 12 (for Windows). You write your GPU kernels once. The Abstraction Boundary (C-ABI) You compile your core engine into a shared library (.so, .dll, .dylib) and expose a flat C-API. This is the boundary where you pass pointers to your model weights and input buffers. The Platform Sub-libraries (Bindings) Once the C-ABI is exposed, you wrap it for your target platforms: Web: Compile the Rust/C++ core to WebAssembly (WASM). The WASM module calls the browser's native WebGPU API. Android: Use JNI (Java Native Interface) to wrap your .so file, exposing it to Kotlin/Java. iOS: Use Swift's C-interop to bind directly to your .dylib. Desktop/Server: Here is where you can use Bun, Deno, Go, or C# .NET. You use their FFI capabilities to call your core engine, handling the high-level orchestration, REST APIs, or WebSocket event-driven pipelines. Key insight: By centralizing the tensor sharding and buffer manipulations inside a Rust/C++ core via wgpu, your platform sub-libraries remain incredibly lightweight. They just handle I/O and UI, while the core does all the heavy math. Want to break down how to structure the WGSL shaders for tensor sharding? Yes yes, give me the structure breakdown for the wgsl shaders for tensor sharding.. also, let's make the application layer more compatible with the deno apis.. because i will be using deno on the application layer for the inference.. make the application abstraction bind to deno for the configs.. for example when the device is an android and the model is too large then the tensor sharding will be more, so that kind of setups and stuff i should be able to do.. if there exists a reusable core engine that is on top of the hardware abstraction with good updates, then let's use that since that will support new architectures when they come in.. but the idea is to have max flexibility If you want maximum flexibility without spending years maintaining raw Vulkan and Metal bindings, you should not build the hardware abstraction from scratch. Instead, use an existing Rust-based machine learning compiler ecosystem like Burn (specifically its CubeCL compiler) or the wgpu crate directly. Burn is particularly powerful for this architecture because it treats wgpu as a first-class citizen and allows you to inject custom shader kernels, while exposing everything through Rust. You can compile this Rust core into a shared library (.so, .dylib, .dll) and orchestrate the entire execution graph from Deno using Deno.dlopen (FFI). Here is how you structure the WGSL shaders and the Deno abstraction layer to handle edge-device limitations. The Edge Sharding Paradigm In a data center, "tensor parallelism" means splitting a matrix across multiple physical GPUs. On edge devices (like an Android phone), you only have one GPU. Here, "tensor sharding" means chunking massive weight matrices into smaller memory fragments to avoid crashing the device's buffer limits. Mobile devices typically have strict maxStorageBufferBindingSize constraints. An Android phone might cap a single GPU buffer at 128MB, whereas a macOS Desktop might allow 2GB. If you try to load a 500MB linear layer weight matrix W on Android, the context will crash. To fix this, Deno must instruct the core to slice W column-wise into smaller shards (e.g., W 1​, W 2​, W 3​, W 4​). The shader runs multiple times, outputting partial activations that are concatenated together. WGSL Structure for Tensor Sharding To keep the engine flexible, the shader should not hardcode matrix dimensions. Instead, it relies on a Uniform buffer to receive the exact dimensions of the current shard being processed. Here is the WGSL architecture for a dynamically sharded matrix multiplication (Y=X⋅W shard​): Code snippet // The configuration injected by Deno -> Rust -> WebGPU Uniform Buffer struct ShardConfig { M: u32, // Sequence length (rows of input) K: u32, // Input features (columns of input) N_shard: u32, // Output features for THIS specific shard stride: u32, // Memory alignment stride } // Group 0: The heavy data buffers @group(0) @binding(0) var inputs: array; @group(0) @binding(1) var weight_shard: array; @group(0) @binding(2) var output_buffer: array; // Group 1: The lightweight dynamic configuration @group(1) @binding(0) var config: ShardConfig; // Workgroup size mapped to hardware warp sizes (usually 32 or 64 on mobile) @compute @workgroup_size(16, 16, 1) fn gemm_shard(@builtin(global_invocation_id) global_id: vec3) { let row = global_id.y; let col = global_id.x; // Bounds checking based on the dynamic shard size if (row >= config.M || col >= config.N_shard) { return; } var sum: f32 = 0.0; // Compute the dot product for this specific chunk for (var k: u32 = 0u; k < config.K; k = k + 1u) { let x_val = inputs[row * config.K + k]; let w_val = weight_shard[k * config.N_shard + col]; sum = sum + (x_val * w_val); } // Write to the correct offset in the output buffer output_buffer[row * config.N_shard + col] = sum; } 3. The Deno FFI Application Layer By putting the configuration logic in Deno, your application layer acts as the "Brain" and the Rust core acts as the "Muscle." When the app boots on a new device, Deno queries the local hardware limits, parses the .safetensors header of the model, calculates the required math to stay under the memory limits, and streams the chunked execution plan into the Rust core. TypeScript // 1. Define the FFI bindings to your Rust core engine const engineCore = Deno.dlopen("./core_engine.so", { "init_gpu_context": { parameters: [], result: "usize" }, "get_max_buffer_size": { parameters: [], result: "usize" }, "load_tensor_shard": { parameters: ["pointer", "usize", "usize"], result: "void" }, "dispatch_gemm_shard": { parameters: ["u32", "u32", "u32"], result: "void" } }); // 2. Query hardware constraints on boot engineCore.symbols.init_gpu_context(); const maxBufferSize = engineCore.symbols.get_max_buffer_size(); // e.g., Android returns 134,217,728 (128MB), Mac returns 2,147,483,648 (2GB) // 3. Dynamic Sharding Logic const totalWeightBytes = 500_000_000; // A 500MB matrix const requiredShards = Math.ceil(totalWeightBytes / maxBufferSize); console.log(Model exceeds buffer limits. Splitting into ${requiredShards} shards.); // 4. Orchestrate the execution pipeline const K = 4096; const N_total = 4096; const N_shard = N_total / requiredShards; for (let i = 0; i < requiredShards; i++) { // Deno reads just the chunk of the safetensor file it needs from disk const chunkPtr = readModelChunkFromDisk(i, N_shard); // Pass the pointer over FFI to Rust to load into the WebGPU buffer engineCore.symbols.load_tensor_shard(chunkPtr, K, N_shard); // Tell Rust to execute the WGSL compute pass for this specific shard engineCore.symbols.dispatch_gemm_shard(1, K, N_shard); } Key insight: Because FFI calls carry a microsecond latency cost, you want Deno to orchestrate at the layer level, not the token level. Deno should pass the entire computation graph for a transformer block to Rust at once, letting the core run the WGSL passes continuously before returning control back to JavaScript. Want to look at the Rust side of the WebGPU memory mapping? Yes Show me how to implement the memory pooling in the Rust core so we aren't constantly allocating and destroying WebGPU buffers between these Deno FFI calls. The cost of allocating a GPU buffer in wgpu is high because it requires a round trip to the OS driver to request physical VRAM mapping. If you do this for every FFI call from Deno, your inference engine will choke on driver overhead rather than actual math. To solve this, the Rust core needs a Buffer Pool Allocator. Instead of creating buffers on demand, Rust pre-allocates a few massive "slab" buffers when the engine boots. When Deno requests memory for a tensor shard, Rust just hands back a logical slice (an offset and a size) within that pre-allocated slab. When Deno is done, Rust marks that slice as free. Rust Architecture for the Pool Here is how you structure this in the Rust core, exposing it to the Deno FFI layer. The Global State Because this core runs as a dynamic library loaded by Deno via FFI, it needs a global state to hold the GPU context and the memory pools. We use lazy_static or std::sync::OnceLock with an atomic pointer to hold the engine. Rust use wgpu; use std::sync::Mutex; use std::collections::BTreeMap; // The global engine instance that Deno will interact with static ENGINE: std::sync::OnceLock> = std::sync::OnceLock::new(); struct InferenceEngine { device: wgpu::Device, queue: wgpu::Queue, pool: BufferPool, } 2. The Buffer Pool Implementation The pool manages the massive VRAM allocations. We use a "Slab" approach. If Deno asks for 64MB, we check if we have a free slice. If not, we allocate a new 128MB slab, give Deno 64MB of it, and keep the rest. Rust struct BufferPool { // The massive pre-allocated VRAM slabs slabs: Vecwgpu::Buffer, // Tracks free memory slices: Map> // BTreeMap keeps it sorted so we can easily find the "best fit" block free_blocks: BTreeMap>, } struct FreeBlock { slab_index: usize, offset: wgpu::BufferAddress, } impl BufferPool { /// Deno asks for memory of size bytes fn allocate(&mut self, device: &wgpu::Device, size: wgpu::BufferAddress) -> PoolAllocation { // 1. Try to find an existing free block that is large enough // We look for the smallest free block that is >= size if let Some((&block_size, blocks)) = self.free_blocks.range_mut(size..).next() { if let Some(block) = blocks.pop() { // If this block is much bigger than what we need, we should split it // and put the remainder back in the free pool. (Skipped here for brevity). return PoolAllocation { slab_index: block.slab_index, offset: block.offset, size, }; } } // 2. If no block is big enough, we must allocate a new Slab on the GPU. // We allocate heavily (e.g., 256MB) to avoid doing this often. let slab_size = size.max(256 * 1024 * 1024); // 256MB minimum let new_buffer = device.create_buffer(&wgpu::BufferDescriptor { label: Some("Tensor Slab"), size: slab_size, // Must support everything we might do with a tensor usage: wgpu::BufferUsages::STORAGE | wgpu::BufferUsages::COPY_DST | wgpu::BufferUsages::COPY_SRC, mapped_at_creation: false, }); let slab_index = self.slabs.len(); self.slabs.push(new_buffer); // Put the leftover memory back in the free pool let leftover = slab_size - size; if leftover > 0 { self.free_blocks.entry(leftover).or_default().push(FreeBlock { slab_index, offset: size, }); } PoolAllocation { slab_index, offset: 0, size, } } /// Deno is done with the memory, put it back in the pool fn free(&mut self, allocation: PoolAllocation) { self.free_blocks.entry(allocation.size).or_default().push(FreeBlock { slab_index: allocation.slab_index, offset: allocation.offset, }); // Note: A production allocator must implement "coalescing" here. // If two adjacent blocks are freed, merge them back into one big block. } } // What Deno actually gets a reference to pub struct PoolAllocation { slab_index: usize, offset: wgpu::BufferAddress, size: wgpu::BufferAddress, } 3. Exposing the API to Deno via FFI Finally, you expose C-compatible functions that Deno can call via dlopen. Notice how we pass back an opaque u64 handle representing the allocation, rather than the Rust struct itself. Rust #[no_mangle] pub extern "C" fn allocate_tensor(size: u64) -> u64 { let mut engine = ENGINE.get().unwrap().lock().unwrap(); let allocation = engine.pool.allocate(&engine.device, size); // Store the allocation in a lookup table and return an ID to Deno let handle_id = engine.store_allocation(allocation); handle_id } #[no_mangle] pub extern "C" fn write_tensor_data(handle_id: u64, data_ptr: *const u8, data_len: u64) { let mut engine = ENGINE.get().unwrap().lock().unwrap(); let allocation = engine.get_allocation(handle_id); let slab_buffer = &engine.pool.slabs[allocation.slab_index]; // Unsafe: we trust Deno that data_ptr is valid memory let data = unsafe { std::slice::from_raw_parts(data_ptr, data_len as usize) }; // Write directly into the specific offset of the massive slab buffer engine.queue.write_buffer(slab_buffer, allocation.offset, data); } #[no_mangle] pub extern "C" fn free_tensor(handle_id: u64) { let mut engine = ENGINE.get().unwrap().lock().unwrap(); let allocation = engine.remove_allocation(handle_id); engine.pool.free(allocation); } Key insight: By managing offsets inside a wgpu::BufferUsages::STORAGE slab, you bypass the OS completely during inference. When you run your WGSL compute shader, you just pass the slab buffer into the bind group and provide the offset to the dispatch_workgroups call. To refine this implementation: How to handle block coalescing when freeing memory How to structure the Ring Buffer for streaming weights include block coalescing when freeing the memory and ring buffer for streaming weights and kv caching... also write the deno implementation for different runtimes like the onnx, gguf, litert, etc... along with file configurations, in the sense the model will be downloaded and stored locally on the fileStorage and loaded when required on to the browser To achieve a production-grade inference engine, we must handle three distinct memory lifecycles: Block Coalescing: Reclaiming fragmented GPU memory by merging adjacent free blocks. Ring Buffers (Weights): Continuously rotating layer weights from disk to RAM to VRAM without blocking computation. KV Caching (Activations): A persistent sliding window in VRAM that scales with sequence length. Rust: Memory Coalescing & Ring Buffers Block Coalescing Logic If you free a 64MB block next to a free 32MB block, they must merge into a 96MB block. To do this efficiently in Rust, you track free blocks in a BTreeMap ordered by their memory offset (not their size), allowing you to easily find adjacent neighbors. Rust use std::collections::BTreeMap; struct SlabAllocator { // Key: Memory Offset, Value: Block Size free_blocks: BTreeMap, total_size: u64, } impl SlabAllocator { pub fn free(&mut self, offset: u64, size: u64) { let mut new_offset = offset; let mut new_size = size; // Check the left neighbor (the block ending exactly at `offset`) if let Some((&left_offset, &left_size)) = self.free_blocks.range(..offset).next_back() { if left_offset + left_size == offset { // Merge left new_offset = left_offset; new_size += left_size; self.free_blocks.remove(&left_offset); } } // Check the right neighbor (the block starting exactly at `offset + size`) if let Some(&right_size) = self.free_blocks.get(&(new_offset + new_size)) { // Merge right new_size += right_size; self.free_blocks.remove(&(new_offset + new_size)); } // Insert the coalesced block self.free_blocks.insert(new_offset, new_size); } } The 3-State Ring Buffer for Streaming To stream a multi-gigabyte model on an edge device, you cannot load the entire .gguf file into VRAM. Instead, you create a ring buffer with three states running concurrently via Rust threads: Read State (VRAM): The GPU is currently executing multiplication against these weights. Write State (RAM): The disk I/O thread is loading the next layer's weights into system memory. Transfer State (PCIe): The driver is DMA-copying the next-next layer from RAM to VRAM. Rust // Rust FFI boundary enum BufferState { Free, LoadingFromDisk, // Handled by Deno/File system ReadyForGPU, // Uploaded to wgpu buffer Computing, // Currently bound to WGSL shader } struct RingBufferChunk { vram_handle: u64, state: BufferState, } // Keep 3 chunks in rotation to mask latency let mut ring = vec![RingBufferChunk::new(); 3]; 2. Deno: Abstraction Layer and File Storage To support different runtimes (ONNX, GGUF, LiteRT) in the browser or via Deno, you need an Abstract Factory pattern. You also need a local file storage strategy — the Origin Private File System (OPFS) is the standard way to handle massive files locally in both Deno and modern browsers without blowing up the Heap. The Runtime Factory Define a standard interface so the main application logic doesn't care whether it's running a .onnx or a .gguf model. TypeScript // core.ts export interface TensorConfig { batch: number; seq_len: number; hidden_size: number; } export interface InferenceRuntime { initialize(modelPath: string): Promise; streamLayer(layerIndex: number): Promise; forwardPass(input: Float32Array): Promise; free(): void; } // Runtimes implement the core interface but handle their specific parsing logic export class GGUFRuntime implements InferenceRuntime { async initialize(modelPath: string) { console.log("Parsing GGUF metadata headers..."); // Read key-value metadata specific to GGUF format } // ... } export class ONNXRuntime implements InferenceRuntime { async initialize(modelPath: string) { console.log("Constructing ONNX protobuf execution graph..."); } // ... } Local Storage and Streaming Logic To stream weights without exceeding browser memory, use the Streams API combined with OPFS. This allows you to fetch a 4GB model from the network, write it directly to the local disk, and then slice it efficiently during inference. TypeScript // storage.ts /** Downloads a model if it doesn't exist, and stores it in the local FileSystem. Uses Streams to avoid loading the entire 4GB file into RAM. */ export async function prepareModelLocal(url: string, filename: string): Promise { const root = await navigator.storage.getDirectory(); // OPFS API try { const fileHandle = await root.getFileHandle(filename); console.log("Model found locally. Skipping download."); return await fileHandle.getFile(); } catch (e) { console.log("Model not found. Downloading directly to local storage..."); // Create the file handle for writing const fileHandle = await root.getFileHandle(filename, { create: true }); const writable = await fileHandle.createWritable(); const response = await fetch(url); if (!response.body) throw new Error("No response body"); // Stream the download directly to disk await response.body.pipeTo(writable); return await fileHandle.getFile(); } } /** Deno orchestration: Reads just the chunk needed for the Ring Buffer */ export async function loadLayerIntoRingBuffer( file: File, offset: number, size: number, coreFfiHandle: any ) { // slice() does not load the file into memory, it just creates a logical view const blob = file.slice(offset, offset + size); const arrayBuffer = await blob.arrayBuffer(); // Pass the raw memory pointer to Rust via FFI const ptr = Deno.UnsafePointer.of(new Uint8Array(arrayBuffer)); coreFfiHandle.symbols.write_tensor_data_to_ring(ptr, size); } Key insight: The File.slice() API is your best friend here. It creates a pointer to a segment of the file on disk, rather than reading it into JavaScript memory. You only pay the memory cost when you call .arrayBuffer() on that specific slice, passing just that small chunk to Rust. Want to look at the KV Cache memory architecture? Yes How do I configure the KV Cache memory buffer in Rust to handle a sliding window of tokens without needing to re-allocate memory for every new token? To achieve a sliding window KV Cache without re-allocating memory, you implement a Circular Buffer (often called a Ring Buffer, though distinct from the weight-streaming ring buffer we discussed earlier). Instead of growing the buffer linearly and re-allocating when you run out of space, you allocate one fixed-size VRAM buffer for your maximum context window upfront. As new tokens generate, a "write pointer" advances. When it hits the end of the buffer, it loops back to index 0, overwriting the oldest tokens. Here is the breakdown of how to structure this across the Rust core and the WGSL shaders. Rust: The Ring Buffer Abstraction In Rust, the KV Cache is fundamentally just a giant block of GPU memory reserved during initialization. You maintain a current_position counter that tracks how many tokens have been processed. Rust pub struct KVCache { pub key_buffer: wgpu::Buffer, pub value_buffer: wgpu::Buffer, pub max_tokens: u32, pub head_dim: u32, pub num_heads: u32, // Total tokens processed across the lifetime of the generation pub absolute_position: u32, } impl KVCache { pub fn new(device: &wgpu::Device, max_tokens: u32, num_heads: u32, head_dim: u32) -> Self { // Calculate size: max_tokens * num_heads * head_dim * size_of(f32) let size = (max_tokens * num_heads * head_dim * 4) as wgpu::BufferAddress; // Allocate ONCE during engine boot or model load let key_buffer = device.create_buffer(&wgpu::BufferDescriptor { label: Some("KV Cache - Keys"), size, usage: wgpu::BufferUsages::STORAGE | wgpu::BufferUsages::COPY_DST, mapped_at_creation: false, }); let value_buffer = device.create_buffer(&wgpu::BufferDescriptor { label: Some("KV Cache - Values"), size, usage: wgpu::BufferUsages::STORAGE | wgpu::BufferUsages::COPY_DST, mapped_at_creation: false, }); Self { key_buffer, value_buffer, max_tokens, head_dim, num_heads, absolute_position: 0, } } /// Returns the physical index in the buffer for the current token pub fn current_physical_index(&self) -> u32 { self.absolute_position % self.max_tokens } } Deno: Writing the New Tokens When a new token is generated, Deno orchestrates the update. The computed K and V vectors for the current token must be written into the cache at the correct physical offset before the attention calculation begins. Rust // Exposed to Deno via FFI #[no_mangle] pub extern "C" fn write_to_kv_cache( handle_id: u64, new_k_ptr: *const f32, new_v_ptr: *const f32 ) { let mut engine = ENGINE.get().unwrap().lock().unwrap(); let cache = engine.get_kv_cache(handle_id); // Calculate where to write in the Ring Buffer let physical_idx = cache.current_physical_index(); // The size in bytes for a single token's K or V vector let token_bytes = (cache.num_heads * cache.head_dim * 4) as u64; let write_offset = (physical_idx as u64) * token_bytes; // Unsafe slice creation from Deno's pointers let k_data = unsafe { std::slice::from_raw_parts(new_k_ptr as *const u8, token_bytes as usize) }; let v_data = unsafe { std::slice::from_raw_parts(new_v_ptr as *const u8, token_bytes as usize) }; // wgpu queue write — this is a fast path, no allocation happens here engine.queue.write_buffer(&cache.key_buffer, write_offset, k_data); engine.queue.write_buffer(&cache.value_buffer, write_offset, v_data); // Increment the position for the NEXT token cache.absolute_position += 1; } 3. WGSL: The Attention Shader The trickiest part of a circular buffer is the math inside the compute shader. During the attention phase (Softmax(Q⋅K T )⋅V), the shader must iterate over all previous tokens. Because the buffer wraps around, the oldest token in the window is no longer at index 0 — it's at (current_physical_index + 1) % max_tokens. The WGSL shader must dynamically calculate the correct offset using the modulo operator. Code snippet struct CacheConfig { max_tokens: u32, absolute_position: u32, head_dim: u32, } @group(0) @binding(0) var config: CacheConfig; @group(0) @binding(1) var key_cache: array; // ... other bindings (Query, Values, Output) ... @compute @workgroup_size(64) fn attention_pass(@builtin(global_invocation_id) global_id: vec3) { // Determine how many tokens are actually in the cache right now // If we haven't wrapped yet, it's absolute_position. If we have, it's max_tokens. let active_tokens = min(config.absolute_position, config.max_tokens); // Where is the "oldest" token currently sitting in the physical buffer? // If we haven't wrapped, the oldest is at 0. // If we have wrapped, the oldest is the one immediately after our current position. var start_idx: u32 = 0u; if (config.absolute_position > config.max_tokens) { start_idx = config.absolute_position % config.max_tokens; } // Now loop over the sequence chronologically for (var i: u32 = 0u; i < active_tokens; i = i + 1u) { // Here is the magic: map the logical sequence index to the physical ring buffer index let physical_idx = (start_idx + i) % config.max_tokens; // Fetch the key vector for this token let key_offset = physical_idx * config.head_dim; // ... proceed with dot product (Q * K) ... } } Key insight: The modulo operation (% config.max_tokens) is what transforms a static array into a sliding window without moving any memory. The GPU simply accesses the data in a wrapped order. Want to optimize the attention pass further? Yes okay.. optimize further and also, keep another kind of an abstraction between the rust and deno such that, from deno i can create parallel agents, and like a framework for harness engineering with looping.. human in the loop, asynchronous runs, tools, etc.. as available in the trending agentic frameworks.. so also confirm that i will be using BURN?? like clarify the entire architecture, the clis, the compilation and runtime Yes, Burn (burn-rs) is absolutely the right choice for the Rust core. If you were building this in 2023, you would have had to manually write wgpu memory allocators and WGSL string concatenations. Today, Burn completely changes the paradigm because of CubeCL — its underlying compute language. With Burn, you write your GPU kernels in standard Rust syntax. CubeCL Just-In-Time (JIT) compiles that Rust code into heavily optimized WGSL (for WebGPU), Vulkan SPIR-V, or CUDA depending on the target device. It natively handles the memory pooling, block coalescing, and dynamic execution graphs we discussed earlier, meaning you don't have to reinvent the wheel. Here is the complete blueprint for your engine, from the high-performance GPU kernels up to the Deno multi-agent orchestration harness. The Core Engine: Burn + CubeCL (Rust) Instead of manually writing .wgsl files, you use CubeCL to write hardware-agnostic kernels. Optimizing the KV Cache with FlashAttention To optimize the attention pass and avoid VRAM memory bottlenecks, you use Block-wise Matrix Multiplication (Tiling). Standard attention calculates the entire N×N matrix, which crashes edge devices. FlashAttention loads small "blocks" or "tiles" of Q, K, and V into the GPU's ultra-fast Shared Memory (Workgroup Memory), computes the partial softmax, and writes the result back to VRAM. Here is how you define a tiled attention kernel using CubeCL in your Rust core: Rust use cubecl::prelude::*; #[cube(launch)] pub fn flash_attention_tiled_kernel( q: &Tensor, k: &Tensor, v: &Tensor, out: &mut Tensor, #[comptime] tile_size: u32, ) { // 1. Allocate ultra-fast Shared Memory (Workgroup memory in WGSL) // This lives on the GPU die, bypassing VRAM latency completely. let mut q_tile = SharedMemory::::new(tile_size * tile_size); let mut k_tile = SharedMemory::::new(tile_size * tile_size); let batch = ABSOLUTE_POS_Z; let head = ABSOLUTE_POS_Y; let seq_idx = ABSOLUTE_POS_X; // 2. Load blocks from VRAM (the Circular Buffer) into Shared Memory // ... logic to copy memory ... // 3. Sync all threads in the workgroup before computing sync_units(); // 4. Compute Q * K^T block locally, apply max/sum for online softmax, // multiply by V block, and accumulate in a register. // ... // 5. Write the final accumulated block back to the `out` VRAM tensor } When Burn runs this on an Android phone, CubeCL compiles it to Vulkan. On a Mac, it compiles to Metal. On the Web, it compiles to WGSL. The Abstraction Boundary: FFI & Compilation Your Rust code is wrapped in a C-ABI, exposing the inference graph to the outside world. The Build Pipeline You use Cargo to cross-compile the Burn engine for your target platforms: Desktop/Server (Deno): cargo build --release --target aarch64-apple-darwin (outputs libengine.dylib or .so). Mobile (Android): cargo ndk -t arm64-v8a build (outputs libengine.so loaded via JNI). Browser: cargo build --target wasm32-unknown-unknown (outputs .wasm bindings). The Deno Application Layer: Agentic Harness Because you are exposing the C-ABI via Deno.dlopen, Deno becomes your orchestrator. This is where you build the Agentic Framework. Deno is uniquely equipped for this because of its native Web Worker support and secure permissions model. You can spin up an isolated Web Worker for every agent, all sharing the same Rust core pointer. The Agentic Loop Architecture A modern agentic harness requires four components: an Inference Pipeline, a Tool Registry, State Memory, and an Asynchronous Event Loop. TypeScript // agent_harness.ts import { coreEngine } from "./ffi_bindings.ts"; export type AgentState = "THINKING" | "WAITING_ON_TOOL" | "WAITING_ON_HUMAN" | "RESPONDING"; export class AgentHarness { private memory: Message[] = []; private tools: Map = new Map(); // Connect via WebSockets for real-time Human-In-The-Loop constructor(private wsClient: WebSocket) {} registerTool(name: string, schema: any, fn: Function) { this.tools.set(name, { schema, fn }); } async run(prompt: string) { this.memory.push({ role: "user", content: prompt }); let state: AgentState = "THINKING"; // The Agentic Loop while (state !== "RESPONDING") { // 1. Ask the Burn Rust core to generate the next sequence const response = await coreEngine.generate(this.memory, this.tools.keys()); if (response.type === "tool_call") { state = "WAITING_ON_TOOL"; // 2. Execute Tool (e.g., query a vector database, call an API) const toolResult = await this.executeTool(response.tool_name, response.args); this.memory.push({ role: "system", content: toolResult }); state = "THINKING"; // Loop back to evaluate tool output } else if (response.type === "human_escalation") { state = "WAITING_ON_HUMAN"; // 3. Human-In-The-Loop (HITL) // Pause execution and emit an event to the frontend UI const humanFeedback = await this.requestHumanIntervention(response.reason); this.memory.push({ role: "user", content: humanFeedback }); state = "THINKING"; // Loop back with human context } else { // 4. Final Generation Stream state = "RESPONDING"; await this.streamToClient(response.text); } } } private requestHumanIntervention(reason: string): Promise { return new Promise((resolve) => { this.wsClient.send(JSON.stringify({ type: "HITL_REQUEST", reason })); // Hook into the WebSocket message listener to resolve this promise // when the human replies via the frontend dashboard. }); } } 4. Scaling the Architecture (Model Context Protocol) Because your application layer is built in Deno, you can easily expose this entire setup as a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server. Instead of hardcoding tools directly into the agent, your Deno harness can connect to an external MCP server over stdio or WebSockets. The Deno orchestrator acts as the client, fetching available tools (like local system commands, database queries, or remote APIs) from the MCP server, serializing them into the system prompt for the Burn inference core, and routing the tool execution requests back out. By pushing the inference math down into a compiled Burn .so file and pulling the orchestration logic up into Deno, you achieve C++ level performance on the GPU while maintaining the incredible developer velocity of TypeScript for complex, event-driven agent architectures. i have attached some repos here as reference for you to understand.. there is deno which is gonna be the layer that will be exposed to the user.. the user can run different kind of file formats on edge devices across cross platform using the native bridge by the burn via rust.. Summary of what landed: Task A — Sampling & runtime polish Logits-processor chain: repetition/frequency/presence penalties → temperature → top-k → top-p (HF-correct semantics) Seeded multinomial sampler (two --seed 42 runs verified byte-identical), greedy applies penalties Stop tokens + boundary-safe stop strings, context-length guard, generation_config.json defaults honored CLI flags: --top-k/--top-p/--repetition-penalty/--seed/--stop/... Task B — Paged KV + engine architecture Attention moved behind the cache (MLC pattern): KVCache::attention(layer, q, k, v, pos, scale) — model code no longer knows how K/V is stored PagedKVCache: page-16 arenas, page table, free-page allocator, popn() rollback; ContiguousKVCache kept as cross-validation baseline (token-identical) Chunked prefill (verified identical to single-shot at 2505 tokens), mpsc worker-thread engine queue (single-flight, Send + Sync, per-token cancellation) Task C — The interesting findings (subagent quota ran out, so I did these by hand) seq≥512 bug root-caused: cubecl's matmul autotuner picks a tile needing 40960B shared memory; Metal allows 32768B; wgpu drops the dispatch silently → zeroed outputs. Worked around via safe_matmul M-slabbing at all sites; repro in tests/attn_cliff.rs. Fused attention experiment — measured, not guessed: I wired attend() through burn's flash-attention (module::attention). Output was token-identical (cubek's bottom-right causal exactly matches chunked-prefill masking), but enabling autotune made decode 3.7× slower (5.6 vs 21 tok/s) — per-process shape-bucket tuning rounds with no persistent cache. So autotune stays OFF; both paths then resolve to the same kernels at full speed (~35 tok/s). The true zero-gather paged decode kernel is documented as future work with a persistent tune cache. Task D — Quantization foundation TensorDtype::U8 + zero-copy int tensor loading combs_core::quant::dequantize_q4 — GGUF q4_0 layout, GPU-side, using portable remainder/div nibble ops (no custom kernel needed) QuantizedLinear — weights stay packed in VRAM (4× footprint cut), dequant + matmul on device; ready for the Phase 5 GGUF adapter Status: 52 tests green, engine at 34.7 tok/s decode / 662 tok/s prefill (1261-token prompt), docs updated. WriteFile README.md IN /Users/sejutisengupta/Projects/CombsEngine/Engine/README.md (overwrite) OUT README.md # Combs Engine > Local-first AI inference engine + agent framework — one Rust GPU core, everywhere. Combs Engine runs large language models **on-device** with a single Rust core compiled for macOS, iOS, Android, Linux, Windows, and Web (WASM) — and exposes it through a C ABI to a full TypeScript agent framework, an OpenAI-compatible server, native mobile shells, and a one-command UI scaffolder. ```bash # Build the CLI cargo xtask build --release # Run a model (HF directory or .gguf file — auto-detected) ./target/release/combs run --model ~/.cache/combs/models/smollm2-135m --chat # Scaffold a full chat UI (Svelte 5, dark/light, auth, permissions) ./target/release/combs chew chat-ui my-app --yes # ...or a multi-agent debate UI ./target/release/combs chew debate-ui my-debate --topic "Pineapple on pizza" --turns 6 # Serve an OpenAI-compatible API ./target/release/combs serve --model ~/.cache/combs/models/smollm2-135m --port 11434 ``` --- ## The Stack Four layers. Compute lives exclusively in **L0** — every layer above is thin orchestration over the same engine. ``` ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ L3 — Platform Shells │ │ Svelte 5 UI template · Android (JNI/Kotlin) · │ │ iOS (Swift) · Web Worker transport │ ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │ L2 — Deno / TypeScript Framework (Engine/Js) │ │ @combs/core · @combs/graph · @combs/agents · │ │ @combs/runtime · @combs/flows · @combs/telemetry │ ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │ L1 — C ABI / JSON-FFI (combs-ffi) │ │ combs_engine_create · combs_chat_completion_stream · │ │ OpenAI-compatible server (`combs serve`) │ ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │ L0 — Rust GPU Core (Engine/Core) │ │ Burn 0.21 · CubeCL · wgpu (Metal/Vulkan/DX12/WebGPU) │ │ Paged KV cache · full sampler · GGUF/SafeTensors │ └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ``` | Layer | Location | What it is | |---|---|---| | L0 Core | [`Core/`](Core) | Cargo workspace: `combs-core`, `combs-formats`, `combs-models`, `combs-runtime`, `combs-ffi`, `combs-cli`, `xtask` | | L2 Framework | [`Js/`](Js) | Deno workspace of 6 `@combs/*` packages | | L3 UI | [`Ui/`](Ui) | Svelte 5 + Vite template consumed by `combs chew` | | L3 Android | [`Android/`](Android) | JNI glue + Kotlin API over the FFI `.so` | | L3 iOS | [`Ios/`](Ios) | Swift wrapper over the FFI static lib | ## What's Available ### L0 — Rust Core (`Engine/Core`) - **Inference engine** — Llama-family architectures on Burn 0.21 + CubeCL/wgpu; validated with SmolLM2 on Apple Silicon (Metal). New architectures = one line in the `ModelRegistry`. - **Model formats** — SafeTensors (Hugging Face layout) and **GGUF v3** (Q8_0 / Q4_0 / F16 / F32) through a single `ModelSource` adapter trait. `combs run --model anything.gguf` just works. - **Memory** — paged KV cache (page-16 arenas, LIFO free-list, prefix-safe `popn`), chunked prefill, quantized linear layers with weights packed in VRAM. - **Sampling** — temperature, top-k, top-p, repetition/frequency/presence penalties, seeded (byte-identical) generation, stop strings & stop tokens. - **CLI (`combs`)** — `run` · `serve` · `devices` · `chew` · `pull` · `convert`. - **OpenAI-compatible server** — `POST /v1/chat/completions` (SSE streaming + non-streaming), `GET /v1/models`, `GET /health`. - **`xtask`** — cross-platform build orchestrator: `cargo xtask matrix` shows live toolchain detection; `cargo xtask bundle` produces `dist//{lib, combs.h}` for every target. ### L2 — TypeScript Framework (`Engine/Js`) | Package | Purpose | |---|---| | `@combs/core` | `Combs.init("smollm2-135m")` — presets, model cache, device planner, FFI / Remote / Worker engines | | `@combs/graph` | LangGraph-equivalent: StateGraph, channels, Pregel runner, checkpointers (Memory/Deno KV/SQLite), human-in-the-loop interrupts, streaming | | `@combs/agents` | Tools & ToolNode, ReAct agents, structured output, memory, MCP client, skills loader | | `@combs/runtime` | Agent HTTP/WS servers, orchestrator, agent pool, KV task queue, session stores | | `@combs/flows` | `createWorkflow` (steps/loops/checks), `createRoleplayChat`, `addMemory` | | `@combs/telemetry` | Scoped logging, OpenTelemetry-shaped spans, metrics | ### L3 — UI & Shells - **`combs chew`** — scaffolder (interactive or fully flag-driven) that stamps out a ready-to-build Svelte 5 app: - 🔐 first-run Ed25519 keypair auth ritual (skippable) - 🛡️ fine-grained network/storage permission grants ("allow once / this session / always") - 📊 realtime network + storage monitor - 🌗 dark/light themes, responsive, chat & multi-agent debate views - **Android** — JNI bridge + Kotlin `CombsEngine` API. - **iOS** — Swift wrapper over the C ABI. - **Web** — `combs-wasm` skeleton + `WorkerEngine` transport (WebGPU enablement in progress). ## Performance Measured on Apple Silicon (wgpu → Metal), SmolLM2-135M-Instruct: | Metric | Value | |---|---| | Decode | ~35 tok/s | | Prefill (1261-token prompt) | ~660 tok/s | | TTFT | ~200 ms | | FFI streaming (via Deno) | ~31 tok/s | ## Model Formats | Format | Status | |---|---| | SafeTensors (HF layout) | ✅ full | | GGUF — F32 / F16 | ✅ full | | GGUF — Q8_0 / Q4_0 | ✅ full | | GGUF — Q4_K / Q5_K / Q6_K | 🚧 planned | Model presets **must** use instruction-tuned variants (e.g. `SmolLM2-135M-Instruct`) — base models echo prompts in chat mode. ## Platform Matrix | Target | Artifact | Status | |---|---|---| | macOS arm64 / x86_64 | `libcombs_ffi.dylib` | ✅ built & tested | | iOS arm64 | `libcombs_ffi.a` | ✅ builds | | Android arm64 | `libcombs_ffi.so` | ✅ builds (NDK) | | Linux / Windows x86_64 | — | ✅ `cargo check` clean (linking needs native toolchain) | | Web (wasm32) | — | 🚧 skeleton, checks clean | Run `cargo xtask matrix` for live detection of your local toolchains. ## Development ```bash # Rust — 53 tests (release profile saves disk) export PATH="/opt/homebrew/opt/rustup/bin:$PATH" # rustup is keg-only on macOS cargo test --release --workspace # TypeScript — 33 tests cd Js && deno task test # Cross-build everything into dist/ cargo xtask bundle ``` Repo conventions live in the root [`AGENTS.md`](../AGENTS.md). ## Contributing Contributions are welcome! To get started: 1. **Fork** the repository and create a branch from `main`. 2. **Keep the layering rule**: L2/L3 only orchestrate — never move compute out of the Rust core (L0). 3. **Match the existing style** — minimal, no external UI/component deps, equivalence-tested. 4. **Add tests** — CPU unit tests with the NdArray backend; GPU tests behind `#[ignore]` + `COMBS_TEST_MODEL`. 5. **Run the full suite** (`cargo test --release --workspace` and `deno task test`) before opening a PR. 6. Open a **pull request** with a clear description of the change. Zero-dep ESM. /** * combs-client — official JS client for Combs Engine servers... */ export class CombsClient { #baseUrl; #fetch; #onDownload; #defaultModel; constructor(options = {}) { const { baseUrl = "http://localhost:8080", fetchImpl, onDownload, model } = options; if (!baseUrl) throw ... this.#baseUrl = baseUrl.replace(/\/+$/, ""); this.#fetch = fetchImpl ?? globalThis.fetch?.bind(globalThis); if (!this.#fetch) throw new Error("no fetch implementation available"); this.#onDownload = onDownload ?? null; this.#defaultModel = model ?? null; } get baseUrl() { return this.#baseUrl; } async #request(path, init = {}) { const res = await this.#fetch(`${this.#baseUrl}${path}`, init); return res; } async listModels() { const res = await this.#request("/v1/models"); if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`HTTP ${res.status}`); const body = await res.json(); this.#onDownload?.(JSON.stringify(body).length); return (body?.data ?? []).map((m) => m.id); } async chatCompletion(request = {}) { // non-streaming const res = await this.#request("/v1/chat/completions", {method: "POST", headers, body: JSON.stringify({... stream:false})}); if (!res.ok) throw ... const body = await res.json(); count; return body?.choices?.[0]?.message?.content ?? ""; } async streamChatCompletion(request = {}, callbacks = {}) { const { onDelta, onDone, onError } = callbacks; try { const res = await this.#request("/v1/chat/completions", {method: "POST", headers: {"content-type": "application/json"}, body: JSON.stringify({messages: request.messages, model: request.model ?? this.#defaultModel, max_tokens: request.maxTokens ?? 256, temperature: request.temperature ?? 0.7, stream: true}), signal: request.signal}); if (!res.ok || !res.body) throw new Error(`HTTP ${res.status}`); ... reader loop same as before, calling this.#onDownload?.(value.byteLength) } catch (err) { onError?.(...) } } } export default CombsClient; Private fields are fine in modern browsers/node. Defaults to global `fetch`. * @param {(bytes: number) => void} [options.onDownload] Received-byte hook. * @param {string} [options.model] Default model id for requests. */ constructor(options = {}) { const { baseUrl = "http://localhost:8080", fetchImpl, onDownload, model, } = options; this.#baseUrl = String(baseUrl).replace(/\/+$/, ""); const f = fetchImpl ?? globalThis.fetch?.bind(globalThis); if (!f) throw new Error("combs-client: no fetch implementation available"); this.#fetch = f; this.#onDownload = onDownload ?? null; this.#defaultModel = model ?? null; } get baseUrl() { return this.#baseUrl; } /** Server health check. @returns {Promise} */ async health() { try { const res = await this.#fetch(`${this.#baseUrl}/health`); return res.ok; } catch { return false; } } /** Lists model ids served by the engine. @returns {Promise} */ async listModels() { const res = await this.#fetch(`${this.#baseUrl}/v1/models`); if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`combs-client: HTTP ${res.status}`); const body = await res.json(); this.#onDownload?.(JSON.stringify(body).length); return (body?.data ?? []).map((m) => m.id); } /** * Non-streaming chat completion. * @param {object} request * @param {{ role: string, content: string }[]} request.messages * @param {string} [request.model] * @param {number} [request.maxTokens=256] * @param {number} [request.temperature=0.7] * @param {AbortSignal} [request.signal] * @returns {Promise} the assistant message content */ async chatCompletion(request = {}) { const res = await this.#fetch(`${this.#baseUrl}/v1/chat/completions`, { method: "POST", headers: { "content-type": "application/json" }, body: JSON.stringify(this.#payload(request, false)), signal: request.signal, }); if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`combs-client: HTTP ${res.status}`); const body = await res.json(); this.#onDownload?.(JSON.stringify(body).length); return body?.choices?.[0]?.message?.content ?? ""; } /** * Streaming chat completion (SSE). Calls `onDelta` per token chunk and * exactly one of `onDone` / `onError` at the end. * @param {object} request Same shape as {@link chatCompletion}. * @param {object} callbacks * @param {(text: string) => void} [callbacks.onDelta] * @param {(finishReason: string) => void} [callbacks.onDone] * @param {(err: Error) => void} [callbacks.onError] */ async streamChatCompletion(request = {}, callbacks = {}) { const { onDelta, onDone, onError } = callbacks; try { const res = await this.#fetch(`${this.#baseUrl}/v1/chat/completions`, { method: "POST", headers: { "content-type": "application/json" }, body: JSON.stringify(this.#payload(request, true)), signal: request.signal, }); if (!res.ok || !res.body) throw new Error(`combs-client: HTTP ${res.status}`); const reader = res.body.getReader(); const decoder = new TextDecoder(); let buffer = ""; for (;;) { const { value, done } = await reader.read(); if (done) break; this.#onDownload?.(value.byteLength); buffer += decoder.decode(value, { stream: true }); const events = buffer.split("\n\n"); buffer = events.pop() ?? ""; for (const raw of events) { const line = raw.trim(); if (!line.startsWith("data:")) continue; const data = line.slice(5).trim(); if (data === "[DONE]") { onDone?.("stop"); return; } const parsed = JSON.parse(data); const delta = parsed?.choices?.[0]?.delta?.content; const finish = parsed?.choices?.[0]?.finish_reason; if (delta) onDelta?.(delta); if (finish) { onDone?.(finish); return; } } } onDone?.("stop"); } catch (err) { onError?.(err instanceof Error ? err : new Error(String(err))); } } #payload(request, stream) { return { messages: request.messages, model: request.model ?? this.#defaultModel ?? undefined, max_tokens: request.maxTokens ?? 256, temperature: request.temperature ?? 0.7, stream, }; } } export default CombsClient; WriteFile index.d.ts WriteFile package.json IN /Users/sejutisengupta/Projects/CombsEngine/Engine/Packages/npm/combs-client/package.json (overwrite) OUT package.json { "name": "combs-client", "version": "0.1.0", "description": "Official JavaScript client for Combs Engine (OpenAI-compatible `combs serve` API). ""; for (const raw of events) { const line = raw.trim(); if (!line.startsWith("data:")) continue; const data = line.slice(5).trim(); if (data === "[DONE]") { cb.onDone("stop"); return; } const parsed = JSON.parse(data); const delta = parsed?.choices?.[0]?.delta?.content; const finish = parsed?.choices?.[0]?.finish_reason; if (delta) cb.onDelta(delta); if (finish) { cb.onDone(finish); return; } } } cb.onDone("stop"); } catch (err) { cb.onError(err instanceof Error ? err : new Error(String(err))); } } const allowed = await permissions.require(scope, detail); if (!allowed) throw new Error(`permission denied: ${detail}`); monitor.netUp(init.body ? String(init.body).length : 0); return fetch(url, init); } /** One CombsClient per server URL, wired to permissions + monitor. */ const clients = new Map(); function clientFor(server: string): CombsClient { let client = clients.get(server); if (!client) { client = new CombsClient({ baseUrl: server, fetchImpl: (url, init) => guardedFetch(url, init ?? {}, "network:inference", `${init?.method ?? "GET"} ${url}`), onDownload: (bytes) => monitor.netDown(bytes), }); clients.set(server, client); } return client; } /** Lists models on the server. */ export function listModels(server: string): Promise { return clientFor(server).listModels(); } /** Streams a chat completion (SSE), calling back per delta. */ export function streamChat( server: string, messages: ChatMessage[], opts: { model?: string; maxTokens?: number; temperature?: number }, cb: StreamCallbacks, signal?: AbortSignal, ): Promise { return clientFor(server).streamChatCompletion({ ...opts, messages, signal }, cb); Proxy backend The scaffolded app needs a backend proxy process: All network traffic (to combs serve, model downloads, anything) goes through the proxy: UI → proxy (localhost) → target. Proxy checks permissions (stored server-side, e.g. a permissions.json file or in-memory with prompts?). How does the proxy ask the user for permission? The permission decisions UI is in the browser... but the module (state, enforcement) lives in the proxy. The proxy can expose an API: when a request hits a scope without a grant, proxy responds 403 with a permission-request id; the frontend shows the dialog (allow once/session/always/deny) and POSTs the decision to the proxy; the proxy stores and enforces. That keeps enforcement + storage on the backend while the UI stays the interaction surface. That's the sensible architecture. File writes also proxied: save-chats etc. go through proxy endpoints (e.g. POST /files/chats) which check storage:write permission and write to disk from the backend. The proxy backend — zero-dependency Node server; all network I/O and file writes go through it, permissions enforced server-side: WriteFile proxy.mjs IN /Users/sejutisengupta/Projects/CombsEngine/Engine/Ui/template/server/proxy.mjs (overwrite) OUT WriteFile dev.mjs Shell mkdir -p /Users/sejutisengupta/Projects/CombsEngine/Engine/Ui/template/server IN mkdir -p /Users/sejutisengupta/Projects/CombsEngine/Engine/Ui/template/server WriteFile proxy.mjs IN /Users/sejutisengupta/Projects/CombsEngine/Engine/Ui/template/server/proxy.mjs (overwrite) OUT proxy.mjs /** * Combs UI proxy — the single network & file gatekeeper for the app. * * The browser NEVER talks to the internet or the disk directly: * - every outbound request goes through POST /api/relay * - every file write/read goes through /api/files/ * and both are permission-checked HERE, on the backend. The frontend's * only job is to show the permission dialog and forward the decision. * * Endpoints: * POST /api/relay {url, method?, headers?, body?, scope, detail?} * → relays bytes upstream (SSE-safe streaming) * → 428 {permissionRequired:{scope,detail}} when ungranted * GET /api/permissions → current grants * POST /api/permissions/decide {scope, grant: once|session|always|deny|reset} * POST /api/files/?scope=storage:chats write body to data/ (gated) * GET /api/files/ read it back * DELETE /api/files/?scope=… delete (gated) * GET /api/monitor {upBytes, downBytes, dataBytes} * * Options: --port (default 8787) --serve (also serve static files) * Zero dependencies; Node >= 18. */ import http from "node:http"; import fs from "node:fs"; import fsp from "node:fs/promises"; import path from "node:path"; import { Readable } from "node:stream"; import { fileURLToPath } from "node:url"; const HERE = path.dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url)); const DATA_DIR = path.join(HERE, "data"); const GRANTS_FILE = path.join(HERE, "permissions.json"); const args = process.argv.slice(2); const PORT = Number(args[args.indexOf("--port") + 1] || 8787); const SERVE_DIR = args.includes("--serve") ? path.resolve(args[args.indexOf("--serve") + 1]) : null; const VALID_GRANTS = new Set(["once", "session", "always", "deny", "reset"]); // --------------------------------------------------------------------------- // Permission store (backend-owned). "always"/"deny" persist to disk; // "session" grants live in memory; "once" allows exactly one attempt. // --------------------------------------------------------------------------- let persisted = {}; try { persisted = JSON.parse(fs.readFileSync(GRANTS_FILE, "utf8")); } catch { /* first run */ } const session = new Map(); // scope -> "session" | "deny" const onceAllowed = new Set(); // scopes approved for a single attempt function checkScope(scope) { if (persisted[scope] === "always") return "allow"; if (persisted[scope] === "deny") return "deny"; if (session.get(scope) === "session") return "allow"; if (session.get(scope) === "deny") return "deny"; if (onceAllowed.has(scope)) { onceAllowed.delete(scope); // consumed return "allow"; } return "ask"; } async function decide(scope, grant) { if (!VALID_GRANTS.has(grant)) return; if (grant === "reset") { delete persisted[scope]; session.delete(scope); onceAllowed.delete(scope); } else if (grant === "always" || grant === "deny") { persisted[scope] = grant; } else if (grant === "session") { session.set(scope, "session"); } else if (grant === "once") { onceAllowed.add(scope); } await fsp.mkdir(DATA_DIR, { recursive: true }); await fsp.writeFile(GRANTS_FILE, JSON.stringify(persisted, null, 2)); } // --------------------------------------------------------------------------- // Monitor counters (the backend sees every byte, so these are the truth). // --------------------------------------------------------------------------- let upBytes = 0; let downBytes = 0; async function dataBytes() { let total = 0; try { for (const f of await fsp.readdir(DATA_DIR)) { total += (await fsp.stat(path.join(DATA_DIR, f))).size; } } catch { /* empty */ } return total; } // --------------------------------------------------------------------------- // HTTP helpers // --------------------------------------------------------------------------- function send(res, status, body, headers = {}) { const isObj = typeof body === "object" && body !== null && !Buffer.isBuffer(body); const payload = isObj ? JSON.stringify(body) : body; res.writeHead(status, { "content-type": isObj ? "application/json" : "application/octet-stream", "access-control-allow-origin": "*", ...headers, }); res.end(payload); } async function readBody(req) { const chunks = []; for await (const c of req) chunks.push(c); return Buffer.concat(chunks); } async function requirePermission(res, scope, detail) { const verdict = checkScope(scope); if (verdict === "allow") return true; if (verdict === "deny") { send(res, 403, { error: `permission denied: ${scope}` }); return false; } // 428 Precondition Required — the frontend shows the dialog, POSTs the // decision to /api/permissions/decide, then retries. send(res, 428, { permissionRequired: { scope, detail } }); return false; } // --------------------------------------------------------------------------- // Handlers // --------------------------------------------------------------------------- async function handleRelay(req, res) { const { url, method = "GET", headers = {}, body = null, scope, detail } = JSON.parse( (await readBody(req)).toString("utf8") || "{}", ); if (!url || !scope) return send(res, 400, { error: "relay needs {url, scope}" }); if (!(await requirePermission(res, scope, detail ?? `${method} ${url}`))) return; upBytes += body ? Buffer.byteLength(body) : 0; let upstream; try { upstream = await fetch(url, { method, headers, body: body ?? undefined, cache: "no-store", // SSE: no buffering }); } catch (e) { return send(res, 502, { error: `upstream unreachable: ${e.message}` }); } const pass = {}; for (const h of ["content-type", "cache-control"]) { const v = upstream.headers.get(h); if (v) pass[h] = v; } res.writeHead(upstream.status, { ...pass, "access-control-allow-origin": "*" }); if (!upstream.body) return res.end(); try { for await (const chunk of Readable.fromWeb(upstream.body)) { downBytes += chunk.length; res.write(chunk); } } catch { /* client aborted */ } res.end(); } function safeName(name) { const clean = path.basename(name); // no traversal if (!clean || clean.startsWith(".")) throw new Error("bad file name"); return clean; } async function handleFiles(req, res, url) { const name = safeName(decodeURIComponent(url.pathname.slice("/api/files/".length))); const file = path.join(DATA_DIR, name); const scope = url.searchParams.get("scope") || "storage:cache"; if (req.method === "GET") { try { const data = await fsp.readFile(file); send(res, 200, data, { "content-type": "application/json" }); } catch { send(res, 404, { error: "not found" }); } return; } if (!(await requirePermission(res, scope, `${req.method} ${name} on this device`))) return; if (req.method === "POST") { const body = await readBody(req); await fsp.mkdir(DATA_DIR, { recursive: true }); await fsp.writeFile(file, body); upBytes += body.length; return send(res, 200, { ok: true, bytes: body.length }); } if (req.method === "DELETE") { await fsp.rm(file, { force: true }); return send(res, 200, { ok: true }); } send(res, 405, { error: "method not allowed" }); } const MIME = { ".html": "text/html", ".js": "text/javascript", ".css": "text/css", ".json": "application/json", ".svg": "image/svg+xml", ".png": "image/png", ".ico": "image/x-icon", ".woff2": "font/woff2", }; async function serveStatic(res, pathname) { const rel = pathname === "/" ? "index.html" : pathname.slice(1); const file = path.join(SERVE_DIR, path.normalize(rel)); if (!file.startsWith(SERVE_DIR)) return send(res, 403, { error: "forbidden" }); try { const data = await fsp.readFile(file); send(res, 200, data, { "content-type": MIME[path.extname(file)] ?? "application/octet-stream" }); } catch { // SPA fallback try { const data = await fsp.readFile(path.join(SERVE_DIR, "index.html")); send(res, 200, data, { "content-type": "text/html" }); } catch { send(res, 404, { error: "not found" }); } } } // --------------------------------------------------------------------------- const server = http.createServer(async (req, res) => { const url = new URL(req.url, `http://localhost:${PORT}`); try { if (req.method === "OPTIONS") { res.writeHead(204, { "access-control-allow-origin": "*", "access-control-allow-methods": "GET,POST,DELETE,OPTIONS", "access-control-allow-headers": "content-type", }); return res.end(); } if (url.pathname === "/api/relay" && req.method === "POST") return await handleRelay(req, res); if (url.pathname === "/api/permissions" && req.method === "GET") { return send(res, 200, { persisted, session: Object.fromEntries(session) }); } if (url.pathname === "/api/permissions/decide" && req.method === "POST") { const { scope, grant } = JSON.parse((await readBody(req)).toString("utf8") || "{}"); if (!scope || !VALID_GRANTS.has(grant)) return send(res, 400, { error: "need {scope, grant}" }); await decide(scope, grant); return send(res, 200, { ok: true }); } if (url.pathname.startsWith("/api/files/")) return await handleFiles(req, res, url); if (url.pathname === "/api/monitor" && req.method === "GET") { return send(res, 200, { upBytes, downBytes, dataBytes: await dataBytes() }); } if (SERVE_DIR) return await serveStatic(res, url.pathname); send(res, 404, { error: "not found" }); } catch (e) { send(res, 500, { error: e.message }); } }); server.listen(PORT, () => { console.log(`[combs-proxy] listening on http://localhost:${PORT}`); console.log(`[combs-proxy] data dir: ${DATA_DIR}`); if (SERVE_DIR) console.log(`[combs-proxy] serving static app from ${SERVE_DIR}`); }); WriteFile dev.mjs Thinking 2. "GET"; return withPermission(scope, `${method} ${url}`, () => fetch("/api/relay", { method: "POST", headers: { "content-type": "application/json" }, body: JSON.stringify({ url, method, headers: init?.headers ?? {}, body: init?.body ?? null, scope, }), signal: init?.signal, }), ); }; } Wait — init.body type is BodyInit; in our use it's a string. Shell rm -rf /Users/sejutisengupta/Projects/CombsEngine/Engine/Ui/template/src/lib/combs-client WriteFile vite.config.ts WriteFile package.json WriteFile api.ts WriteFile permissions.ts IN /Users/sejutisengupta/Projects/CombsEngine/Engine/Ui/template/src/lib/permissions.ts (overwrite) OUT permissions.ts /** * Fine-grained permissions: every sensitive action (network downloads, * local caching) asks first — allow once / allow this session / allow * always / deny. Grants are persisted per scope in localStorage; session * grants live in memory only . */ export type Grant = "once" | "session" | "always" | "deny"; /** * Permissions — the frontend half. All grant state and ALL enforcement * live in the backend proxy (server/proxy.mjs); this store only renders * the ask-dialog and forwards the user's decision to the proxy, which * persists it and applies it to every relayed request / file write. */ export type Grant = "once" | "session" | "always" | "deny"; permissions.ts export type PermissionScope = | "network:download" // downloading models over the internet | "network:inference" // talking to the inference server | "storage:cache" // caching models/chats on this device | "storage:chats"; // persisting chat sessions interface PendingRequest { export type PermissionScope = | "network:download" // downloading models over the internet | "network:inference" // talking to the inference server | "storage:cache" // caching data on this device | "storage:chats"; // persisting chat sessions interface PendingRequest { permissions.ts resolve: (grant: Grant) => void; } const KEY = "combs.permissions"; class PermissionStore { /** Pending permission request shown in the dialog (reactive). */ pending = $state(null); private persisted: Record = JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem(KEY) ?? "{}"); private session = new Map(); private onceUsed = new Set(); /** Checks a scope; shows the dialog when no grant exists. Resolves true * when the action is allowed this time. */ async require(scope: PermissionScope, detail: string): Promise { const always = this.persisted[scope]; if (always === "always") return true; if (always === "deny") return false; const sessionGrant = this.session.get(scope); if (sessionGrant === "session") return true; if (sessionGrant === "deny") return false; if (this.onceUsed.has(scope)) { // "allow once" was already consumed — ask again. this.onceUsed.delete(scope); } const grant = await new Promise((resolve) => { this.pending = { scope, detail, resolve }; }); this.pending = null; switch (grant) { case "always": this.persisted[scope] = grant; localStorage.setItem(KEY, JSON.stringify(this.persisted)); return true; case "session": this.session.set(scope, grant); return true; case "once": this.onceUsed.add(scope); return true; case "deny" : default: this.session.set(scope, "deny"); return false; } } /** Current persisted grants (for the settings panel). */ grants(): Record{ return { ...this.persisted}; } revoke(scope: PermissionScope): void{ delete this.persisted[scope]; this.session.delete(scope); localStorage.setItem(KEY, JSON.stringify(this.persisted)); } } resolve: (grant: Grant) => void; } class PermissionStore { /** Pending permission request shown in the dialog (reactive). */ pending = $state(null); /** * Shows the dialog, POSTs the decision to the backend proxy, and * resolves true when this attempt may proceed (caller then retries). */ async ask(scope: PermissionScope, detail: string): Promise { const grant = await new Promise((resolve) => { this.pending = { scope, detail, resolve }; }); this.pending = null; try { await fetch("/api/permissions/decide", { method: "POST", headers: { "content-type": "application/json" }, body: JSON.stringify({ scope, grant }), }); } catch { // proxy unreachable — treat as denied return false; } return grant !== "deny"; } /** Current grants as known by the backend (for the settings panel). */ async grants(): Promise> { try { const res = await fetch("/api/permissions"); if (res.ok) { const body = await res.json(); return { ...body.persisted, ...body.session }; } } catch { // proxy unreachable } return {}; } /** Clears a grant server-side. */ async revoke(scope: PermissionScope): Promise { try { await fetch("/api/permissions/decide", { method: "POST", headers: { "content-type": "application/json" }, body: JSON.stringify({ scope, grant: "reset" }), }); } catch { // proxy unreachable } } } WriteFile monitor.ts WriteFile chat.ts Thinking 3. combs-client: remove the hardcoded default — baseUrl is now required; README updated since the template uses the real package: StrReplaceFile index.js StrReplaceFile index.d.ts StrReplaceFile README.md Thinking 4. chew.rs — model mandatory, auth non-negotiable (no flag, no bypass): StrReplaceFile chew.rs while scaffolding the chat-ui and agent-ui using chew we had a logic like if the user selects a model, or if uses a flag without model, then no auth.. let's make it simple.. no need for that.. user has to select a model and no configuration can escape the auth... let's have like a proxy on top of the application that will relay all the network connection.. whenever data has to come in or go out, it has to be through that proxy... this proxy is what will check for the permissions... similarly for file writes also, we had asked for permissions.. the permission module should not live onthe frontend, but on the backend server and should monitor the permissions... remove any hardcodings on the npm package.. i'll release it now and let's fetch it from the real package and serve, if the lib was hardcoded.. walk me through the instructions on releasing packages on all platforms.. make sure agent-ui is also setup properly K Kimi Thinking StrReplaceFile chew.rs Shell cd /Users/sejutisengupta/Projects/CombsEngine/Engine/Core && export PATH="/opt/homebrew/opt/rustup/bin:$PATH" && cargo build --release -p combs-cli 2>&1 | grep -E "^error" -A5; Writing the plan: WriteFile ravager-red-star-elongated-man.md IN /Users/sejutisengupta/.kimi/plans/ravager-red-star-elongated-man.md (overwrite) OUT ravager-red-star-elongated-man.md # Zero-Trust Security Layer for Combs UI & Agents ## Library answer (researched) - **Passkeys**: [`@simplewebauthn/browser` + `@simplewebauthn/server`](https://github.com/MasterKale/SimpleWebAuthn) **v13.3.2** — exactly the right choice. ESM, TypeScript-first, runs on Node 20+, Deno 1.43+/2.x, Bun; published to both npm and JSR. Actively maintained (v13.3.2, June 2026, incl. a security fix). - **Everything else: NO library needed — built-in WebCrypto**. `crypto.subtle` is the *same API* in browsers, Node ≥19 (`globalThis.crypto`), and Deno. It covers every primitive we need: ECDSA P-256 (signatures — already used in `auth.ts`), ECDH P-256 (key agreement), HKDF-SHA256, AES-256-GCM (confidentiality + integrity tag), SHA-256 (hashes). One isomorphic module runs in all three runtimes — no NaCl/libsodium/tweetnacl dependency. ## Threat model / principles 1. The browser main tab holds the **root identity**. Nothing else is trusted by default. 2. Every byte in/out crosses a **proxy**; every proxy runs the **zero-trust middleware** (encrypt-at-rest, integrity-verify-on-read, guardrail hooks). 3. **Permissions are enforced server-side** (already done) and **approval now requires a passkey assertion**. An existing grant skips the passkey prompt, but *never* skips the crypto integrity layer. 4. Agents (subprocess tabs) communicate only in **sealed envelopes**: ECDH(ephemeral) → HKDF → AES-256-GCM, SHA-256 hash of plaintext inside, ECDSA signature outside. Peers are keyed once (permission-checked); subsequent messages need no permission. 5. MCP/tool internet access gets a **sandboxed proxy** with the token1/token2 swap (below). ## Architecture ``` main tab (browser) ┌─────────────── proxy (Node, template) ───────────────┐ identity: ECDSA P-256 (sign) │ /api/auth/passkey/* SimpleWebAuthn ceremonies │ + ECDH P-256 (encrypt) ───▶│ /api/relay → guard → upstream (net) │ passkey (WebAuthn, device-bound) │ /api/files → encrypt+manifest → disk │ │ passkey assertion │ /api/agents/* → key registry + envelope relay │ ▼ │ /api/sandbox/* → mint token1/token2, spawn sandbox │ PermissionDialog (approve w/ passkey)└───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │ env/WS (token-authed, existing) ▼ ▼ agent subprocess (Deno, @combs/runtime) sandboxed proxy (per grant) own ECDH keypair; sealed envelopes token1 decrypt → guardrail → fetch via Orchestrator WS → guardrail → token2 encrypt → reply ``` ### Crypto core — new package `combs-zerotrust` `Engine/Packages/npm/combs-zerotrust/` — isomorphic plain-JS (browser/Node/Deno), zero-dep, WebCrypto-only. Mirrored to JSR as `@combs/zerotrust` (single source in `Engine/Js/zerotrust/mod.js`; npm copy at publish, documented in RELEASING.md — same pattern as the chew vendor step). - `identity` — generate/load {sign: ECDSA P-256, enc: ECDH P-256}; fingerprint = SHA-256(pubkeys). - `seal/open(from, toPub, plaintext)` — envelope `{v, from, eph, nonce, ct, sig}`: ephemeral ECDH → HKDF → AES-256-GCM; ciphertext embeds SHA-256(plaintext) (integrity hash); ECDSA signature over the envelope (sender auth). `open` verifies sig + hash + GCM tag; any tamper → hard fail. - `keystore` — AES-256-GCM master key (per app, wrapped by identity-enc key); per-blob data keys wrapped by master; manifest entries `{location → {nonce, wrappedKey, sha256}}`. - `tokens` — mint/validate 256-bit capability tokens (token1/token2), TTL + bound agentId; possession = capability. ### Phase A — encrypted storage middleware (proxy + localStorage) - `server/zerotrust.mjs` (template): middleware wrapping `/api/files/*` — POST encrypts (per-file key, wrapped; hash+nonce into `server/manifest.json`), GET decrypts and verifies hash; mismatch → 409 + quarantine. `/api/relay` downloads (model blobs) stream through a tee → encrypted at rest in `data/models/` with manifest entry; reads decrypt+verify before serving to the engine. - Frontend `secureStore.ts`: localStorage values sealed (AES-GCM, key = HKDF from identity-enc private key) + hash; transparent wrapper replacing raw `localStorage` in theme/permissions remnants/chat meta. - Validation: curl round-trip shows ciphertext on disk, correct plaintext back, tamper → 409. ### Phase B — passkey approval (SimpleWebAuthn) - Template gains `@simplewebauthn/browser` (frontend) + `@simplewebauthn/server` (proxy) deps. (Proxy is no longer zero-dep — declared in package.json.) - Proxy `server/authn.mjs`: `POST /api/auth/passkey/register-options|register-verify|auth-options|auth-verify`; credentials in `server/authn.json`; challenges TTL 5 min in memory. RP ID = localhost; HTTPS/localhost required by WebAuthn. - Frontend: after existing keypair ritual (`auth.ts`), AuthSetup gains "Create passkey" (startRegistration; credential ID signed by identity key for binding). PermissionDialog: instead of plain buttons → "Approve with passkey" (startAuthentication → assertion → `/api/auth/passkey/auth-verify` → then `/api/permissions/decide`). Deny stays click-only. - Existing grants/capability session → no passkey re-prompt (per requirement), crypto layer still applied. - Validation: unit-test verify endpoints with a test credential; manual ceremony on localhost (real Touch ID). ### Phase C — agent subprocess key exchange + E2E messaging - `AgentPool.spawn` (Engine/Js/runtime): after health-check, hand the **main identity's enc-pubkey** to the subprocess over its token-authed channel (`POST /keys`, never in argv/logs); agent generates its own ECDH pair and `POST /keys` back. Both sides record peer pubkey under agentId → permission prompt (passkey) fires **once per agent initialization**; never again for that agent. - Orchestrator WS messages become sealed envelopes (`combs-zerotrust.seal/open`). Main tab ↔ agent traffic is encrypted+hashed+signed in both directions; replies need no new permission (peer already keyed). - Validation: Deno test — two agents exchange tampered/untampered envelopes; tamper rejected. ### Phase D — sandboxed proxy for MCP/tools (token1/token2 flow) Exactly the specified flow, `server/sandbox.mjs`: 1. Agent requests `network:internet` → forwarded to main proxy → **passkey permission** on main tab. 2. On approve: main proxy mints `token1` (agent→sandbox key) + `token2` (sandbox→main key), spawns sandboxed proxy (domain allowlist, method allowlist, size caps, secret-pattern denylist, full audit log), stores `token2` against agentId, returns `token1` to the agent. 3. Agent adopts `token1` as its credential for internet calls (replacing pubkey on that channel), sends token1-sealed requests → sandbox decrypts, **guardrail-checks**, fetches upstream. 4. Response guardrail-checked → sealed with `token2` → main agent decrypts with the `token2` it saved at approval. Tampered data fails GCM → dropped, never reaches the tab. - Guardrails phase 1 = deterministic rules; phase 2 (noted, not built) = local-model judge via `combs serve`. ## File changes | Area | Files | |---|---| | New crypto pkg | `Engine/Js/zerotrust/{mod.js,deno.json}`, `Engine/Packages/npm/combs-zerotrust/*` | | Template proxy | `server/zerotrust.mjs`, `server/authn.mjs`, `server/sandbox.mjs`, edits to `server/proxy.mjs` (middleware wiring), `package.json` (deps) | | Template frontend | `src/lib/auth.ts` (passkey step), `src/lib/components/PermissionDialog.svelte` (passkey approve), new `src/lib/secureStore.ts`, `src/lib/permissions.ts` (assertion flow) | | Deno runtime | `Engine/Js/runtime/src/orchestrator.ts` (key handoff + envelope transport), new `runtime/src/keys.ts` | | Docs | `Engine/README.md`, `AGENTS.md`, `Engine/Packages/RELEASING.md` (combs-zerotrust) | ## Validation - Node smoke: seal/open round-trip, tamper rejection, keystore wrap/unwrap (like the combs-client test). - curl suite against proxy: encrypted file round-trip, manifest tamper → 409, passkey endpoints with test credential, sandbox token flow with mock upstream. - `deno task test` (33) stays green + new envelope tests; template `vite build`; `combs chew` end-to-end. ## Explicit non-goals (documented, future) Passkey PRF-derived master keys, model-judge guardrails, multi-device passkey sync/recovery, attestation trust anchors.
